It leaves me feeling completely unrefreshed by the morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually have enough time and opportunity to sleep, or are there nights when you simply cannot sleep even though your schedule would allow it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely have the time and opportunity to sleep, but my mind simply won't let me. I go to bed around 11 PM intending to rest, but I end up lying awake until 1 or 2 in the morning because my brain is constantly racing with worries about my mom and my work responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Besides the sleep problem, do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, such as work, family, finances, or your health, and feeling unable to control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind is constantly scanning for what could go wrong, whether it's my mother's health, a server crash at work, or even just a missed bill. I try to tell myself to stop, but the worries just keep looping and multiplying, constantly interrupting my attempts to focus on my Bible study or crochet.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get physical symptoms like restlessness, muscle tension, feeling on edge, fatigue, trouble concentrating, or irritability?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel wound tight all the time, constantly pacing or tapping my foot like I'm bracing for an emergency, and I've been snapping at my husband and brother over tiny things. I also feel constantly drained, and my concentration is so poor that I find myself rereading the same reports at work because I just can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, overwhelming panic attacks or things like a racing heart or dizziness. For me, it's more of a constant, daily state of tension and worry that just never seems to let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have unwanted, intrusive thoughts that you feel driven to neutralize with repeated behaviors or rituals, like checking, counting, or washing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like washing, counting, or checking. My mind definitely loops through worries and contingency plans constantly, but I don't perform any physical actions to try to stop them."

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I just can’t seem to get a full night’s sleep anymore, my mind won’t let me rest and I wake up feeling exhausted. I try to tell myself to stop, but the worries just keep looping and multiplying, constantly interrupting my attempts to focus on my Bible study or crochet.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get physical symptoms like restlessness, muscle tension, feeling on edge, fatigue, trouble concentrating, or irritability?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel wound tight all the time, constantly pacing or tapping my foot like I'm bracing for an emergency, and I've been snapping at my husband and brother over tiny things. I also feel constantly drained, and my concentration is so poor that I find myself rereading the same reports at work because I just can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, overwhelming panic attacks or things like a racing heart or dizziness. For me, it's more of a constant, daily state of tension and worry that just never seems to let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have unwanted, intrusive thoughts that you feel driven to neutralize with repeated behaviors or rituals, like checking, counting, or washing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like washing, counting, or checking. My mind definitely loops through worries and contingency plans constantly, but I don't perform any physical actions to try to stop them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I have pulled back from things I normally love, like my Saturday walking club, crocheting, and church activities. It is not that I feel deeply hopeless, but I am just too physically exhausted from the lack of sleep to participate. My mind is also so distracted by constant worries and feeling on edge that I can't seem to focus on or enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unable to sleep even when you are relaxed and have enough time to rest, or is the main problem that worries keep your mind active and prevent you from falling asleep?"

She sought psychiatric evaluation after her husband and church choir director both commented that she seemed unusually tired and withdrawn, and she recognized that her difficulties getting restful sleep had been going on for months and were starting to interfere with both her job focus and her ability to be present with her family.\n- Chief Complaint: “I just can’t seem to get a full night’s sleep anymore, my mind won’t let me rest and I wake up feeling exhausted.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie there from 11 until one or two in the morning, my brain running through backup plans for the city’s network or imagining my mom falling in the bathroom, and even when I finally do doze off I keep jolting awake every couple of hours and get up feeling like I never really slept. \n- S056 (Excessive_Anxiety_Worry): It feels like my mind is constantly scanning for what could go wrong—my mother’s blood pressure, a server crashing during a storm, whether I forgot to pay some bill—so even small things, like a missed call from the clinic or an email from my boss, set off this chain of worst‑case scenarios in my head. \n- S057 (Uncontrollable_Worry): I’ll tell myself, ‘Stop, Daniela, this is enough,’ but the worries just keep looping and multiplying, so that trying to focus on a Bible study, a crochet pattern, or even a TV show gets interrupted every few minutes by another “what if” barging in. \n- S058 (Restlessness_Tension): Lately I feel like I’m wound tight all the time, tapping my foot during meetings, pacing in the kitchen while coffee brews, and unable to just sit and enjoy my crochet or a walk with the ladies because my body feels like it’s bracing for some emergency call. \n- S060 (Irritability): Because I’m so on edge, I snap at my husband over little things—like him misplacing the remote or my brother being five minutes late to relieve me with my mom—and then I feel awful afterward because I know they didn’t do anything that really deserved that reaction. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: At work I’ve started rereading the same network reports because I can’t stay focused, and at home I’ve pulled back from my walking group and church activities because I feel too drained and distracted to really participate.\n- duration: Looking back, this constant worrying and feeling on edge has been building and pretty much ongoing since my mom’s health crises really started ramping up about a year ago.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I just can’t seem to get a full night’s sleep anymore, my mind won’t let me rest and I wake up feeling exhausted.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: Chief Complaint, S027 (Sleep_Disturbance), S056 (Excessive_Anxiety_Worry), S058 (Restlessness_Tension)\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that your sleep trouble is almost always because your mind is racing with worries and your body feels tense. Explain that you lie awake with your brain running through backup plans for work or imagining worst-case scenarios about your mother, and that you rarely have nights where you actually feel calm enough to test if you could sleep without those worries."

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I just can’t seem to get a full night’s sleep anymore, my mind won’t let me rest and I wake up feeling exhausted. My mind definitely loops through worries and contingency plans constantly, but I don't perform any physical actions to try to stop them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I have pulled back from things I normally love, like my Saturday walking club, crocheting, and church activities. It is not that I feel deeply hopeless, but I am just too physically exhausted from the lack of sleep to participate. My mind is also so distracted by constant worries and feeling on edge that I can't seem to focus on or enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unable to sleep even when you are relaxed and have enough time to rest, or is the main problem that worries keep your mind active and prevent you from falling asleep?\n\nPatient: The main problem is definitely my mind racing with worries, as I rarely feel relaxed lately because my body is always wound so tight. As soon as I lie down, my brain immediately starts running through backup plans for work or worrying about my mother's health, making it impossible to drift off.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?"

It is almost always because my mind is racing with worries and my body feels so tense. I lie awake running through backup plans for work or imagining worst-case scenarios about my mother. I go to bed around 11 PM intending to rest, but I end up lying awake until 1 or 2 in the morning because my brain is constantly racing with worries about my mom and my work responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Besides the sleep problem, do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, such as work, family, finances, or your health, and feeling unable to control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind is constantly scanning for what could go wrong, whether it's my mother's health, a server crash at work, or even just a missed bill. I try to tell myself to stop, but the worries just keep looping and multiplying, constantly interrupting my attempts to focus on my Bible study or crochet.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get physical symptoms like restlessness, muscle tension, feeling on edge, fatigue, trouble concentrating, or irritability?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel wound tight all the time, constantly pacing or tapping my foot like I'm bracing for an emergency, and I've been snapping at my husband and brother over tiny things. I also feel constantly drained, and my concentration is so poor that I find myself rereading the same reports at work because I just can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, overwhelming panic attacks or things like a racing heart or dizziness. For me, it's more of a constant, daily state of tension and worry that just never seems to let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have unwanted, intrusive thoughts that you feel driven to neutralize with repeated behaviors or rituals, like checking, counting, or washing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like washing, counting, or checking. My mind definitely loops through worries and contingency plans constantly, but I don't perform any physical actions to try to stop them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I have pulled back from things I normally love, like my Saturday walking club, crocheting, and church activities. It is not that I feel deeply hopeless, but I am just too physically exhausted from the lack of sleep to participate. My mind is also so distracted by constant worries and feeling on edge that I can't seem to focus on or enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unable to sleep even when you are relaxed and have enough time to rest, or is the main problem that worries keep your mind active and prevent you from falling asleep?\n\nPatient: The main problem is definitely my mind racing with worries, as I rarely feel relaxed lately because my body is always wound so tight. As soon as I lie down, my brain immediately starts running through backup plans for work or worrying about my mother's health, making it impossible to drift off.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?\n\nPatient: It is almost always because my mind is racing with worries and my body feels so tense. I lie awake running through backup plans for work or imagining worst-case scenarios about my mother. I honestly can't remember the last time I felt calm enough at night to even see if I could sleep without those worries.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?"

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I just can’t seem to get a full night’s sleep anymore, my mind won’t let me rest and I wake up feeling exhausted. I struggle with both; I lie awake until 1 or 2 in the morning with my mind racing about my mom and my work, and even when I do drift off, I keep waking up every couple of hours. It leaves me feeling completely unrefreshed by the morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually have enough time and opportunity to sleep, or are there nights when you simply cannot sleep even though your schedule would allow it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely have the time and opportunity to sleep, but my mind simply won't let me. I go to bed around 11 PM intending to rest, but I end up lying awake until 1 or 2 in the morning because my brain is constantly racing with worries about my mom and my work responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Besides the sleep problem, do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, such as work, family, finances, or your health, and feeling unable to control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind is constantly scanning for what could go wrong, whether it's my mother's health, a server crash at work, or even just a missed bill. I try to tell myself to stop, but the worries just keep looping and multiplying, constantly interrupting my attempts to focus on my Bible study or crochet.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get physical symptoms like restlessness, muscle tension, feeling on edge, fatigue, trouble concentrating, or irritability?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel wound tight all the time, constantly pacing or tapping my foot like I'm bracing for an emergency, and I've been snapping at my husband and brother over tiny things. I also feel constantly drained, and my concentration is so poor that I find myself rereading the same reports at work because I just can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, overwhelming panic attacks or things like a racing heart or dizziness. For me, it's more of a constant, daily state of tension and worry that just never seems to let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have unwanted, intrusive thoughts that you feel driven to neutralize with repeated behaviors or rituals, like checking, counting, or washing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like washing, counting, or checking. My mind is also so distracted by constant worries and feeling on edge that I can't seem to focus on or enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unable to sleep even when you are relaxed and have enough time to rest, or is the main problem that worries keep your mind active and prevent you from falling asleep?\n\nPatient: The main problem is definitely my mind racing with worries, as I rarely feel relaxed lately because my body is always wound so tight. As soon as I lie down, my brain immediately starts running through backup plans for work or worrying about my mother's health, making it impossible to drift off.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?\n\nPatient: It is almost always because my mind is racing with worries and my body feels so tense. I lie awake running through backup plans for work or imagining worst-case scenarios about my mother. I honestly can't remember the last time I felt calm enough at night to even see if I could sleep without those worries.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?\n\nPatient: It really is entirely because of the worries and tension; I don't have nights where I feel calm but still can't sleep. I lie awake for hours with my mind running through backup plans for work or worrying about my mother falling. Even when I do finally drift off, I keep jolting awake every couple of hours because my body is so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries and tension been present more days than not for at least 6 months, and have they made it hard to work, manage home life, or enjoy your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, this constant worry and tension has been going on almost every day for about a year now, ever since my mother's health crises began. It has definitely impacted my life, making it hard to focus at work where I constantly have to reread network reports, and causing me to pull back from my church activities and walking group because I'm just too drained and distracted to participate.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had a persistently low mood, or felt hopeless or worthless most days?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or worthless at all. It is more that I feel extremely exhausted, overwhelmed, and constantly anxious or worried about everything going on around me."

    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"F41.1\", \"F51.0\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I just can’t seem to get a full night’s sleep anymore, my mind won’t let me rest and I wake up feeling exhausted. It has gotten to the point where I'm just too tired to function properly during the day.\n\nDoctor: How long has this sleep problem been going on, and do you have trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early?\n\nPatient: This has been going on for about a year now, starting around the time my mother's health began to decline. I struggle with both; I lie awake until 1 or 2 in the morning with my mind racing about my mom and my work, and even when I do drift off, I keep waking up every couple of hours. It leaves me feeling completely unrefreshed by the morning.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually have enough time and opportunity to sleep, or are there nights when you simply cannot sleep even though your schedule would allow it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I absolutely have the time and opportunity to sleep, but my mind simply won't let me. I go to bed around 11 PM intending to rest, but I end up lying awake until 1 or 2 in the morning because my brain is constantly racing with worries about my mom and my work responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Besides the sleep problem, do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different things most days, such as work, family, finances, or your health, and feeling unable to control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mind is constantly scanning for what could go wrong, whether it's my mother's health, a server crash at work, or even just a missed bill. I try to tell myself to stop, but the worries just keep looping and multiplying, constantly interrupting my attempts to focus on my Bible study or crochet.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worrying, do you also get physical symptoms like restlessness, muscle tension, feeling on edge, fatigue, trouble concentrating, or irritability?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel wound tight all the time, constantly pacing or tapping my foot like I'm bracing for an emergency, and I've been snapping at my husband and brother over tiny things. I also feel constantly drained, and my concentration is so poor that I find myself rereading the same reports at work because I just can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had sudden episodes of intense fear or panic that peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, dizziness, or feeling like something terrible is about to happen?\n\nPatient: No, I don't experience those sudden, overwhelming panic attacks or things like a racing heart or dizziness. For me, it's more of a constant, daily state of tension and worry that just never seems to let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have unwanted, intrusive thoughts that you feel driven to neutralize with repeated behaviors or rituals, like checking, counting, or washing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like washing, counting, or checking. My mind definitely loops through worries and contingency plans constantly, but I don't perform any physical actions to try to stop them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I have pulled back from things I normally love, like my Saturday walking club, crocheting, and church activities. It is not that I feel deeply hopeless, but I am just too physically exhausted from the lack of sleep to participate. My mind is also so distracted by constant worries and feeling on edge that I can't seem to focus on or enjoy them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unable to sleep even when you are relaxed and have enough time to rest, or is the main problem that worries keep your mind active and prevent you from falling asleep?\n\nPatient: The main problem is definitely my mind racing with worries, as I rarely feel relaxed lately because my body is always wound so tight. As soon as I lie down, my brain immediately starts running through backup plans for work or worrying about my mother's health, making it impossible to drift off.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?\n\nPatient: It is almost always because my mind is racing with worries and my body feels so tense. I lie awake running through backup plans for work or imagining worst-case scenarios about my mother. I honestly can't remember the last time I felt calm enough at night to even see if I could sleep without those worries.\n\nDoctor: When you have trouble sleeping, is it mainly because your mind is racing with worries and tension, or do you also have nights when you simply cannot sleep even when you feel calm and not particularly worried?\n\nPatient: It really is entirely because of the worries and tension; I don't have nights where I feel calm but still can't sleep. I lie awake for hours with my mind running through backup plans for work or worrying about my mother falling. Even when I do finally drift off, I keep jolting awake every couple of hours because my body is so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries and tension been present more days than not for at least 6 months, and have they made it hard to work, manage home life, or enjoy your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, this constant worry and tension has been going on almost every day for about a year now, ever since my mother's health crises began. It has definitely impacted my life, making it hard to focus at work where I constantly have to reread network reports, and causing me to pull back from my church activities and walking group because I'm just too drained and distracted to participate.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you also had a persistently low mood, or felt hopeless or worthless most days?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel hopeless or worthless at all. It is more that I feel extremely exhausted, overwhelmed, and constantly anxious or worried about everything going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping even on nights when you feel calm and not particularly worried, or is the sleep problem only when your mind is racing with anxiety and tension?\n\nPatient: I rarely have nights where I feel truly calm anymore because I feel wound tight all the time. My sleep issues are almost always accompanied by my mind racing with worries about my mother's health or work contingencies, making it impossible to rest."

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.
